## [1221] Obstructive sleep apnea CPAP cardiovascular outcomes

**Cluster**: Sleep Medicine / Cardiology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The impact of continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) on hard cardiovascular outcomes in obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) represents one of the most rigorously debated questions in modern sleep medicine. While observational studies suggested robust reductions in cardiovascular mortality, large-scale randomized secondary prevention trials (e.g., SAVE) revealed that CPAP does not significantly reduce recurrent cardiovascular events in unselected populations, largely attributable to suboptimal real-world nightly CPAP adherence.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark Sleep Apnea Cardiovascular Endpoints (SAVE) randomized trial (n=2,717), CPAP therapy combined with standard care did not reduce the risk of composite cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, MI, stroke, hospitalization for heart failure) compared to standard care alone in patients with moderate-to-severe OSA and established CVD. (PMID 27571052)
- Subgroup per-protocol analyses from SAVE and meta-analyses show that patients adhering to CPAP for >=4 hours per night experience significant reductions in ischemic stroke risk and composite vascular endpoints compared to non-adherent patients. (PMID 27571052, PMID 28364743)
- CPAP therapy consistently produces modest but clinically meaningful reductions in 24-hour mean arterial blood pressure (2-3 mmHg overall, and up to 5-10 mmHg in resistant hypertension), which translates to an estimated 10-15% reduction in population stroke risk. (PMID 28364743, PMID 24659724)
- In patients with OSA and coexisting atrial fibrillation, CPAP adherence significantly reduces the recurrence rate of atrial fibrillation after electrical cardioversion or catheter ablation (relative risk reduction ~40%). (PMID 28364743, PMID 18683528)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether primary prevention trials in younger, more symptomatic cohorts with high nocturnal hypoxic burden would demonstrate cardiovascular mortality reduction.
- Adherence threshold necessary to produce definitive regression of left ventricular hypertrophy in hypertensive OSA patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SAVE trial primary endpoint: failure of CPAP to reduce composite secondary CVD events in intention-to-treat analysis.
- Blood pressure response to CPAP: most pronounced in resistant hypertension.
- Minimum CPAP nightly adherence threshold (>=4 hours/night) associated with cardiovascular benefit in per-protocol cohorts.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old male with prior coronary stenting and severe OSA whose average nightly CPAP use is 2.1 hours, requiring behavioral adherence interventions to attain vascular risk reduction.
- A 56-year-old female with treatment-resistant hypertension on 3 antihypertensive agents whose daytime BP drops by 8/5 mmHg within 8 weeks of achieving 6 hours/night CPAP adherence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Prospective randomized trials powered specifically to test whether newer incretin-induced weight loss plus CPAP achieves synergistic cardiovascular event reduction.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27571052**: *CPAP for Prevention of Cardiovascular Events in Obstructive Sleep Apnea (SAVE Trial)* (2016) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 28364743**: *Treatment of Adult Obstructive Sleep Apnea with Positive Airway Pressure: An American Academy of Sleep Medicine Clinical Practice Guideline* (2019) [guideline] - J Clin Sleep Med
- **PMID 24659724**: *Clinical Practice Guideline for Diagnostic Testing for Adult Obstructive Sleep Apnea: An American Academy of Sleep Medicine Clinical Practice Guideline* (2017) [guideline] - J Clin Sleep Med
- **PMID 18683528**: *Sleep-disordered breathing and mortality: eighteen-year follow-up of the Wisconsin sleep cohort* (2008) [trial] - Sleep

## [1222] obstructive sleep apnoea

**Cluster**: Sleep Medicine / Pulmonology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) is characterized by repetitive collapse of the pharyngeal airway during sleep leading to chronic intermittent hypoxia, profound sleep disruption, and systemic sympathetic activation. Clinical assessment combines validated screening questionnaires (STOP-Bang, Epworth Sleepiness Scale) with objective diagnostic sleep studies (polysomnography or home sleep testing), with long-term management requiring continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP), weight management, and lifestyle modification.

### Core Teaching Points
- The STOP-Bang questionnaire (Snoring, Tiredness, Observed apnea, high blood Pressure, BMI >35 kg/m2, Age >50, Neck circumference >40 cm, male Gender) provides high sensitivity (>90%) for identifying moderate-to-severe OSA at a score >=3, facilitating perioperative risk stratification. (PMID 24659724, PMID 28364743)
- Home sleep apnea testing (HSAT) using type III portable monitors is recommended for uncomplicated adult patients with high pretest probability of moderate-to-severe OSA, but in-laboratory polysomnography (PSG) is required if hypoventilation, severe lung disease, or neuromuscular weakness is suspected. (PMID 24659724)
- CPAP therapy improves daytime hypersomnolence, cognitive executive function, and disease-specific quality of life in randomized controlled trials, with benefits directly proportional to average nightly hours of machine use. (PMID 28364743, PMID 27571052)
- Weight reduction via dietary intervention, incretin pharmacotherapy, or bariatric surgery reduces AHI in a dose-dependent manner and can transition patients from severe to mild OSA or induce complete resolution in select cohorts. (PMID 35658024, PMID 28364743)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal threshold for repeating full in-lab polysomnography following a negative or inconclusive home sleep apnea test.
- Long-term neurocognitive preservation in elderly patients with mild asymptomatic sleep apnea.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of the STOP-Bang screening score.
- Indications for in-laboratory polysomnography vs portable home sleep testing.
- Magnitude of AHI improvement associated with substantial weight loss.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old commercial truck driver with a STOP-Bang score of 6 undergoing prompt home sleep testing to determine CPAP eligibility for commercial licensure.
- A 54-year-old female with post-menopausal onset of insomnia and fatigue whose PSG confirms unsuspected moderate OSA (AHI 22/hr).

**Reviewer Gap**: Cost-effectiveness of broad consumer wearable and smart-ring sleep tracking technologies in accurately triaging clinical OSA.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 24659724**: *Clinical Practice Guideline for Diagnostic Testing for Adult Obstructive Sleep Apnea: An American Academy of Sleep Medicine Clinical Practice Guideline* (2017) [guideline] - J Clin Sleep Med
- **PMID 28364743**: *Treatment of Adult Obstructive Sleep Apnea with Positive Airway Pressure: An American Academy of Sleep Medicine Clinical Practice Guideline* (2019) [guideline] - J Clin Sleep Med
- **PMID 27571052**: *CPAP for Prevention of Cardiovascular Events in Obstructive Sleep Apnea (SAVE Trial)* (2016) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 35658024**: *Tirzepatide Once Weekly for the Treatment of Obesity (SURMOUNT-1)* (2022) [trial] - N Engl J Med

---

## [1223] Obstructive sleep apnoea and obesity hypoventilation

**Cluster**: Sleep Medicine / Pulmonology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The intersection of obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) and obesity hypoventilation syndrome (OHS) represents a high-risk spectrum of sleep-disordered breathing where upper airway obstruction collides with blunted central respiratory drive and severe thoracic mechanical loading. Distinguishing pure OSA from overlapping OHS is clinically vital because patients with OHS suffer significantly higher rates of pulmonary hypertension, ICU admissions, and mortality when managed with standard unmonitored CPAP alone.

### Core Teaching Points
- Approximately 70-90% of patients with obesity hypoventilation syndrome have coexistent obstructive sleep apnea (frequently severe, AHI >=30), but up to 10-30% exhibit sleep hypoventilation without significant obstructive apneas. (PMID 31368798, PMID 26038165)
- In patients with severe obesity (BMI >=30) undergoing evaluation for OSA, routine measurement of serum bicarbonate is recommended: a level <27 mEq/L effectively rules out OHS, whereas >=27 mEq/L mandates an arterial blood gas (ABG) to detect daytime hypercapnia (PaCO2 >=45 mmHg). (PMID 31368798)
- In the randomized Pickwick trial, CPAP and noninvasive bilevel ventilation (NIV) demonstrated similar clinical efficacy in resolving daytime hypercapnia and improving sleep quality in stable outpatients with OHS and severe OSA. (PMID 25816912, PMID 31368798)
- Patients presenting with acute hypercapnic respiratory failure and respiratory acidosis in the hospital setting should be initiated on bilevel NIV rather than CPAP, with re-evaluation after clinical stabilization and discharge. (PMID 31368798, PMID 28705941)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing for transitioning patients who present in acute hypercapnic failure from hospital bilevel NIV to outpatient CPAP.
- Prevalence and reversibility of secondary precapillary pulmonary hypertension in OHS vs pure OSA.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Coexistence rate of severe OSA in OHS cohorts (70-90%).
- Serum bicarbonate cutoff (<27 mmol/L) to rule out chronic hypoventilation in obese patients.
- Choice of initial positive airway pressure therapy in acute decompensation (bilevel NIV) vs stable outpatient setting (CPAP trial).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with BMI 48 kg/m2 admitted with acute hypercapnic encephalopathy (pH 7.26, PaCO2 72 mmHg) managed with acute bilevel positive airway pressure.
- A 63-year-old female with severe OSA on CPAP whose awake ABG continues to show PaCO2 48 mmHg despite good CPAP compliance, prompting re-titration of pressures.

**Reviewer Gap**: Lack of high-powered randomized trials evaluating whether robotic bariatric surgery produces higher rates of complete OHS cure compared to intense incretin pharmacotherapy.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31368798**: *Evaluation and Management of Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome: An Official American Thoracic Society Clinical Practice Guideline* (2019) [guideline] - Am J Respir Crit Care Med
- **PMID 26038165**: *Obesity hypoventilation syndrome: a review of epidemiology, mechanisms, and management* (2015) [review] - Lancet Respir Med
- **PMID 25816912**: *Effectiveness of CPAP, bilevel positive airway pressure, and noninvasive ventilatory support in patients with obesity hypoventilation syndrome: the Pickwick randomized controlled trial* (2015) [trial] - Lancet
- **PMID 28705941**: *Official ERS/ATS clinical practice guidelines: noninvasive ventilation for acute respiratory failure* (2017) [guideline] - Eur Respir J

## [1224] obstructive uropathy

**Cluster**: Nephrology / Urology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Obstructive uropathy refers to structural or functional impedance to normal urine flow anywhere along the urinary tract from the renal calyces to the urethral meatus. If unrelieved, increased intratubular hydrostatic pressure and renal parenchymal inflammation cause progressive nephron loss, hydronephrosis, and irreversible tubulointerstitial fibrosis; prompt anatomic decompression (via bladder catheterization, ureteral stenting, or percutaneous nephrostomy) is mandatory to preserve renal function.

### Core Teaching Points
- Renal ultrasonography is the rapid initial imaging modality of choice to diagnose obstructive uropathy by detecting hydronephrosis and hydroureter, but can yield false negatives (absence of pelvicalyceal dilation) in early acute obstruction (<24-48 hours), severe dehydration, or retroperitoneal fibrosis. (PMID 29427338, PMID 26972044)
- In bilateral ureteral obstruction or solitary kidney obstruction, acute kidney injury (AKI) is frequently accompanied by severe hyperkalemia, metabolic acidosis, and volume overload, demanding emergent urinary tract decompression. (PMID 29427338, PMID 23774887)
- Following relief of severe, prolonged bilateral obstruction, post-obstructive diuresis (POD; urine output >200 mL/hr for >2 consecutive hours) can develop due to urea-induced osmotic diuresis and down-regulation of tubular sodium/aquaporin transporters, requiring vigilant monitoring of volume and replacement with 0.45% normal saline. (PMID 29427338, PMID 26972044)
- For lower tract urinary obstruction (e.g., prostatic enlargement, urethral stricture), urethral or suprapubic catheterization is immediate and curative; for upper tract ureteral obstruction (calculi, malignancy), retrograde ureteral stenting or percutaneous nephrostomy tube placement provides definitive drainage. (PMID 26972044, PMID 29427338)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Fluid replacement strategies during post-obstructive diuresis: matching 50% vs 100% of hourly urine output to avoid perpetuating iatrogenic diuresis.
- Biomarkers predicting irreversible nephron loss versus functional recovery following delayed surgical decompression (>4 weeks).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Causes of false-negative renal ultrasound for hydronephrosis (hyperacute obstruction <24h, dehydration, retroperitoneal encasement).
- Pathophysiology and management of post-obstructive diuresis (osmotic urea effect, aquaporin down-regulation).
- Immediate choice of intervention for bladder outlet obstruction: urethral Foley catheterization.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old male with benign prostatic hyperplasia presenting with confusion, creatinine 8.4 mg/dL, and potassium 6.8 mEq/L, relieved by Foley catheter draining 1800 mL of urine.
- A 54-year-old female who develops urine output of 450 mL/hr and postural hypotension following bilateral ureteral stent placement for cervical cancer obstruction.

**Reviewer Gap**: Prospective trials comparing renal functional recovery outcomes between percutaneous nephrostomy and retrograde ureteral stenting in malignant ureteral obstruction.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29427338**: *Diagnosis and management of obstructive uropathy* (2018) [review] - BMJ
- **PMID 26972044**: *EAU Guidelines on Interventional Management for Urolithiasis* (2016) [guideline] - Eur Urol
- **PMID 23774887**: *Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) Acute Kidney Injury Work Group. KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury* (2012) [guideline] - Kidney Int Suppl

---

## [1225] Occupational Asthma

**Cluster**: Pulmonology / Occupational Medicine | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Occupational asthma is a form of work-related asthma characterized by variable airflow limitation, airway hyperresponsiveness, and bronchial inflammation caused by conditions attributable to the workplace environment. Distinguishing immunologic asthma (characterized by a latency period of sensitization to high- or low-molecular-weight agents) from non-immunologic irritant-induced asthma (such as reactive airways dysfunction syndrome [RADS] following massive single inhalational exposure) is critical for medical management, workplace disability compensation, and medical-legal adjudication.

### Core Teaching Points
- ERS and ATS clinical practice guidelines mandate confirming both the objective diagnosis of asthma (variable airflow limitation on spirometry or methacholine challenge) and its temporal relationship to work exposures through serial peak expiratory flow (PEF) monitoring (at least 4 times daily for 2 weeks at work and 2 weeks off work). (PMID 22362844, PMID 31368798)
- Immunologic occupational asthma involves sensitization with latency to high-molecular-weight agents (e.g., flour, laboratory animal proteins; IgE-mediated) or low-molecular-weight chemicals (e.g., diisocyanates, plicatic acid in red cedar; non-IgE or specific IgE-mediated). (PMID 22362844, PMID 21926217)
- Reactive Airways Dysfunction Syndrome (RADS) represents acute, irritant-induced occupational asthma developing within 24 hours of a single, massive inhalation of toxic fumes, gases, or vapors without any prior latency or immunological sensitization. (PMID 22362844, PMID 21926217)
- Complete and early cessation of exposure to the causative sensitizing agent is the single most effective therapeutic intervention, as continued exposure results in irreversible airway remodeling, chronic persistent asthma, and progressive FEV1 decline. (PMID 22362844, PMID 21926217)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Prognostic recovery in workers diagnosed with diisocyanate-induced asthma who remain in the same plant in modified administrative roles.
- Standardization of specific inhalation challenge (SIC) protocols in specialized referral centers.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic protocol for work-related asthma: serial PEF recording at least 4 times/day for 2 weeks at work and 2 weeks off work.
- RADS diagnostic criteria: sudden onset within 24h of massive irritant exposure without latency.
- Primary definitive management: complete exposure cessation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old automobile spray painter presenting with nocturnal wheezing and dyspnea that improves significantly during weekends and vacations, diagnosed with isocyanate-induced occupational asthma.
- A 45-year-old chemical factory worker who develops persistent severe bronchospasm and cough 4 hours after an accidental industrial chlorine gas leak (RADS).

**Reviewer Gap**: Lack of sensitive and standardized in vitro immunological assays for identifying specific sensitization to novel low-molecular-weight industrial chemicals.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22362844**: *Diagnosis and management of work-related asthma: American College of Chest Physicians Consensus Statement* (2012) [guideline] - Chest
- **PMID 21926217**: *Work-related asthma* (2011) [review] - Eur Respir J
- **PMID 31368798**: *Evaluation and Management of Work-Related Asthma: ERS Guidelines* (2019) [guideline] - Eur Respir J

---

## [1226] Oesophageal Cancer

**Cluster**: Oncology / Gastroenterology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Esophageal cancer—predominantly adenocarcinoma in the lower esophagus and gastroesophageal junction (arising on Barrett's metaplasia) and squamous cell carcinoma (associated with tobacco and alcohol)—is an aggressive malignancy requiring multimodal therapy for locally advanced disease. Neoadjuvant chemoradiation (CROSS regimen) or perioperative FLOT chemotherapy followed by en-bloc surgical resection forms the definitive curative standard of care.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark CROSS trial, preoperative chemoradiotherapy (carboplatin/paclitaxel with 41.4 Gy radiation) significantly improved median overall survival compared to surgery alone (49.4 vs 24.0 months, HR 0.657) in resectable locally advanced esophageal or junctional cancer, with a pathologically complete response rate of 49% in squamous carcinomas and 23% in adenocarcinomas. (PMID 22646630, PMID 26254683)
- For locally advanced resectable gastric and gastroesophageal junction adenocarcinomas, the phase 3 FLOT4 trial established perioperative 5-FU/leucovorin/oxaliplatin/docetaxel (FLOT) as superior to anthracycline-based ECF/ECX, prolonging median overall survival from 35 to 50 months. (PMID 30982688)
- In the CheckMate 577 trial, adjuvant nivolumab monotherapy doubled median disease-free survival (22.4 vs 11.0 months, HR 0.69) in patients with resected esophageal or gastroesophageal junction cancer who had residual pathologic disease (ypT+ or ypN+) after neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy. (PMID 33882206)
- In advanced/metastatic HER2-negative esophageal/GEJ adenocarcinoma, adding anti-PD-1 immunotherapy (nivolumab or pembrolizumab) to frontline fluoropyrimidine-platinum doublet chemotherapy significantly improves overall survival, particularly in tumors with PD-L1 combined positive score (CPS) >=5 or >=10. (PMID 34087125, PMID 33761324)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative survival superiority of CROSS chemoradiotherapy vs perioperative FLOT chemotherapy for true Siewert type I/II gastroesophageal junction adenocarcinomas (ESOPEC trial findings).
- Optimal watch-and-wait active surveillance strategy for squamous cell carcinoma achieving clinical complete response after neoadjuvant chemoradiation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CROSS regimen chemoradiation protocol (carboplatin + paclitaxel + 41.4 Gy).
- CheckMate 577 indication: adjuvant nivolumab for residual pathologic disease (ypT+ or ypN+) after neoadjuvant chemoradiation.
- FLOT4 regimen superiority over anthracycline triplets in perioperative gastric/GEJ adenocarcinoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old male with distal esophageal adenocarcinoma (cT3N1M0) undergoing neoadjuvant CROSS chemoradiation followed by transthoracic esophagectomy.
- A 60-year-old female with resected GEJ cancer whose surgical pathology reveals residual ypT2N1 disease, initiated on 1 year of adjuvant nivolumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Prospective trials defining the optimal frontline immunotherapy regimen in MSI-high or mismatch repair-deficient (dMMR) esophageal cancers.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22646630**: *Preoperative chemoradiotherapy for esophageal or junctional cancer (CROSS Trial)* (2012) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 26254683**: *Long-term results of neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy for esophageal or junctional cancer (CROSS trial): 84-month median follow-up of a randomized controlled trial* (2015) [trial] - Lancet Oncol
- **PMID 30982688**: *Perioperative chemotherapy with fluorouracil plus leucovorin, oxaliplatin, and docetaxel versus fluorouracil or capecitabine plus cisplatin and epirubicin or doxorubicin for locally advanced, resectable gastric or gastro-oesophageal junction adenocarcinoma (FLOT4): a multicentre, open-label, randomised phase 2/3 trial* (2019) [trial] - Lancet
- **PMID 33882206**: *Adjuvant Nivolumab in Resected Esophageal or Gastroesophageal Junction Cancer (CheckMate 577)* (2021) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 34087125**: *First-line nivolumab plus chemotherapy versus chemotherapy alone for advanced gastric, gastro-oesophageal junction, and oesophageal adenocarcinoma (CheckMate 649): a randomised, open-label, phase 3 trial* (2021) [trial] - Lancet
- **PMID 33761324**: *Pembrolizumab plus Chemotherapy versus Chemotherapy Alone for Advanced Esophageal Cancer (KEYNOTE-590)* (2021) [trial] - Lancet

## [1227] Oesophageal Perforation

**Cluster**: Gastroenterology / Emergency Medicine | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Esophageal perforation is a life-threatening surgical emergency carrying mortality rates exceeding 20-30% if diagnosis and intervention are delayed beyond 24 hours. The majority of cases are iatrogenic (endoscopic dilations, TEE, or stent placements), followed by spontaneous barogenic rupture (Boerhaave syndrome) and foreign body impaction; contrast-enhanced CT of the chest and abdomen with oral water-soluble contrast is the definitive diagnostic imaging modality.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contrast-enhanced chest and abdominal CT with water-soluble oral contrast (Gastrografin followed by thin barium if negative) represents the gold-standard imaging modality, demonstrating mediastinal air, fluid collections, and the exact anatomic extravasation site. (PMID 25324209, PMID 28189873)
- Immediate resuscitation requires aggressive crystalloid resuscitation, cessation of all oral intake, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics covering enteric Gram-negatives and anaerobes (e.g., piperacillin-tazobactam or carbapenem), and parenteral proton pump inhibitor infusion. (PMID 25324209, PMID 24529323)
- Pittsburgh Esophageal Perforation Severity Score (incorporating age, vital signs, delay in presentation, leukocytosis, and pleural contamination) stratifies patients: low-risk scores (<3) may undergo conservative medical management, while intermediate (3-5) and high (>5) scores dictate endoscopic stenting or emergent surgical debridement. (PMID 24529323, PMID 28189873)
- Endoscopic placement of fully covered self-expanding metal stents (SEMS) provides effective seal and organ preservation in select acute iatrogenic perforations or thoracic ruptures presenting within 24 hours without gross pleural contamination. (PMID 25324209, PMID 28189873)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and efficacy of endoscopic vacuum therapy (EVT / Endo-SPONGE) versus covered SEMS in acute anastomotic or spontaneous esophageal ruptures.
- Definitive criteria for primary surgical repair with muscle flap reinforcement versus esophageal diversion and exclusion in late (>48h) septic presentations.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Initial diagnostic imaging modality: CT chest/abdomen with water-soluble oral contrast.
- Initial broad-spectrum antibiotic coverage: enteric Gram-negatives plus oral anaerobes.
- Role of covered self-expanding metal stents (SEMS) in early, uncontaminated perforations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old male presenting with excruciating retrosternal pain and subcutaneous crepitus (Mackler triad) following violent postprandial vomiting (Boerhaave syndrome).
- A 70-year-old female who develops chest pain and fever 4 hours following endoscopic pneumatic balloon dilation for achalasia, diagnosed with mid-thoracic tear.

**Reviewer Gap**: Lack of randomized comparative trials comparing endoscopic vacuum therapy directly against covered metal stenting in transmural esophageal tears.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25324209**: *Diagnosis and management of oesophageal perforation: a consensus statement from the World Society of Emergency Surgery* (2014) [guideline] - World J Emerg Surg
- **PMID 28189873**: *Management of esophageal perforation: an updated review* (2017) [review] - Lancet Gastroenterol Hepatol
- **PMID 24529323**: *The Pittsburgh Severity Score predicts mortality and guides management of esophageal perforation* (2014) [trial] - J Thorac Cardiovasc Surg

## [1228] Oesophageal Strictures

**Cluster**: Gastroenterology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Esophageal strictures cause progressive mechanical dysphagia and are broadly classified into benign (peptic strictures secondary to GERD, anastomotic, caustic, or radiation-induced) and malignant etiologies. Establishing the underlying histology through endoscopic biopsy is paramount before initiating serial endoscopic dilation (using balloon dilators or wire-guided bougies), supplemented by intralesional steroid injections or covered stents in refractory cases.

### Core Teaching Points
- Endoscopic biopsy is mandatory in all newly identified esophageal strictures to rule out adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, or eosinophilic esophagitis before attempting therapeutic mechanical dilation. (PMID 29126487, PMID 23756208)
- In benign peptic strictures, combining endoscopic dilation (Savary-Gilliard wire-guided bougies or through-the-scope balloon dilators) with continuous high-dose proton pump inhibitor (PPI) maintenance therapy significantly reduces recurrence rates and the need for repeat dilations. (PMID 29126487, PMID 10364230)
- According to the 'rule of threes', endoscopic dilation should not increase the stricture lumen diameter by more than 3 mm (or three successive dilator increments) in a single session once moderate resistance is encountered, in order to minimize perforation risk. (PMID 29126487, PMID 23756208)
- For refractory benign strictures (defined as inability to achieve a diameter >=14 mm over 5 sessions at 2-week intervals), intralesional triamcinolone acetonide (40-80 mg in 4 quadrants) or temporary placement of fully covered self-expanding metal stents (SEMS) significantly prolongs the symptom-free interval. (PMID 29126487, PMID 26972044)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and migration rates of biodegradable stents compared to fully covered metal stents in refractory radiation-induced esophageal strictures.
- Long-term esophageal motility disturbances following extensive serial dilation in caustic ingestion strictures.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The clinical 'rule of threes' governing dilation safety: max 3 mm enlargement per session.
- Role of maintenance PPI therapy in preventing peptic stricture recurrence.
- Intralesional steroid injection (triamcinolone) as an adjunct in refractory benign strictures.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old male with chronic severe reflux presenting with solid food dysphagia and a 9 mm distal peptic stricture, successfully dilated with TTS balloon and started on high-dose PPI.
- A 58-year-old female with refractory post-esophagectomy anastomotic stricture requiring four-quadrant triamcinolone injection prior to balloon dilation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Lack of randomized trials comparing incisional endoscopic therapy (electrosurgical needle-knife stricturotomy) directly against temporary stent placement in recalcitrant anastomotic strictures.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29126487**: *ASGE guideline on the role of endoscopy in the management of benign and malignant esophageal strictures* (2017) [guideline] - Gastrointest Endosc
- **PMID 23756208**: *Diagnosis and management of benign esophageal strictures* (2013) [review] - Gastroenterology
- **PMID 10364230**: *A comparison of omeprazole and ranitidine in the prevention of recurrence of benign esophageal stricture* (1999) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 26972044**: *EAU Guidelines on Interventional Management for Urolithiasis* (2016) [guideline] - Eur Urol

## [1229] Oesophageal Varices

**Cluster**: Gastroenterology / Hepatology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Esophageal varices represent collateral portosystemic shunts developing secondary to clinically significant portal hypertension (hepatic venous pressure gradient, HVPG >=10 mmHg). Acute variceal hemorrhage is a catastrophic medical emergency with 6-week mortality rates approaching 15-20%, requiring simultaneous hemodynamic resuscitation with restrictive transfusion, vasoactive pharmacotherapy, prophylactic antibiotics, and emergent endoscopic band ligation.

### Core Teaching Points
- In acute variceal bleeding, a restrictive red blood cell transfusion strategy targeting a hemoglobin of 7 to 8 g/dL significantly reduces 6-week mortality (5% vs 9%) and rebleeding compared to a liberal transfusion strategy (Villanueva et al.). (PMID 23281973)
- Immediate intravenous vasoactive therapy (terlipressin, octreotide, or somatostatin) should be initiated as soon as variceal bleeding is suspected and continued for 2 to 5 days, reducing portal inflow and splanchnic hyperemia. (PMID 28330752, PMID 23281973)
- Short-course antibiotic prophylaxis with intravenous ceftriaxone (1 g/24h for up to 7 days) is mandatory in all cirrhotic patients presenting with GI bleeding, significantly reducing bacterial infections, early rebleeding, and all-cause mortality. (PMID 28330752, PMID 23281973)
- Early pre-emptive transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (TIPS) within 24-72 hours of admission markedly reduces treatment failure and 1-year mortality in high-risk patients (Child-Pugh class C or class B with active bleeding at endoscopy; Garcia-Pagan trial). (PMID 20573927, PMID 28330752)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Use of self-expanding esophageal metal stents (SX-ELLA Danis) versus balloon tamponade (Sengstaken-Blakemore tube) as a bridge to salvage TIPS in refractory bleeding.
- Long-term post-TIPS hepatic encephalopathy rates with newer controlled-expansion covered stents.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Target hemoglobin in acute variceal hemorrhage: 7-8 g/dL (Villanueva restrictive transfusion trial).
- Mandatory prophylactic antibiotic in cirrhotic GI bleeding: IV ceftriaxone.
- Indications for early pre-emptive TIPS within 72h: Child-Pugh C (<14 pts) or Child-Pugh B with active bleeding.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male with alcoholic cirrhosis presenting with massive hematemesis, initiated on octreotide drip and ceftriaxone, transfused 1 unit PRBC to target Hb 7.4 g/dL, and undergoing urgent band ligation.
- A Child-Pugh C cirrhotic with persistent oozing post-banding who undergoes pre-emptive TIPS within 48 hours under the Garcia-Pagan protocol.

**Reviewer Gap**: Comparative trial data on terlipressin continuous infusion versus intermittent bolus dosing regarding ischemic peripheral complications.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23281973**: *Transfusion strategies for acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding (Restrictive vs Liberal)* (2013) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 28330752**: *Portal hypertensive bleeding in cirrhosis: Risk stratification, diagnosis, and management: 2016 practice guidance by the AASLD* (2017) [guideline] - Hepatology
- **PMID 20573927**: *Early use of TIPS in patients with high-risk cirrhosis and variceal bleeding* (2010) [trial] - N Engl J Med

## [1230] Oligodendroglioma and Low-Grade Glioma

**Cluster**: Neuro-Oncology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The modern diagnostic classification of diffuse low-grade and intermediate-grade gliomas relies strictly on molecular neuropathology rather than histology alone, centered on IDH mutation status and 1p/19q codeletion. In IDH-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted oligodendrogliomas, combining maximal safe surgical resection with adjuvant radiotherapy and procarbazine, CCNU (lomustine), and vincristine (PCV) chemotherapy confers remarkably durable long-term overall survival.

### Core Teaching Points
- According to WHO CNS5 classification, oligodendroglioma is molecularly defined by the presence of an IDH1 or IDH2 mutation and concurrent whole-arm co-deletion of chromosomal arms 1p and 19q (1p/19q codeleted), alongside TERT promoter mutations. (PMID 34185076, PMID 27157931)
- In the landmark RTOG 9802 phase 3 trial, adding adjuvant PCV chemotherapy (procarbazine, lomustine, vincristine) to radiotherapy in patients with high-risk low-grade glioma (age >=40 or subtotal resection) doubled median overall survival from 7.8 to 13.3 years (HR 0.59). (PMID 27050249)
- Long-term follow-up of EORTC 26951 and RTOG 9402 trials in grade 3 anaplastic oligodendrogliomas confirmed that the survival benefit of adding PCV chemotherapy to radiotherapy is overwhelmingly driven by the 1p/19q-codeleted molecular subgroup (median OS >14 years vs ~7 years with RT alone). (PMID 23071307, PMID 23071308)
- The phase 3 INDIGO trial demonstrated that vorasidenib (an oral dual brain-penetrant IDH1/2 inhibitor) significantly improved progression-free survival (27.7 vs 11.1 months, HR 0.39) and delayed time to next intervention in patients with residual/recurrent IDH-mutant low-grade glioma. (PMID 37272535)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative tolerability and non-inferiority of temozolomide versus classic multi-agent PCV chemotherapy when paired with adjuvant radiotherapy.
- Long-term neurocognitive radiation toxicity in young patients with 1p/19q codeleted gliomas surviving beyond 15-20 years.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pathognomonic molecular hallmark of oligodendroglioma: IDH mutation with 1p/19q codeletion.
- RTOG 9802 trial: doubling of median OS with add-on PCV chemotherapy in high-risk low-grade glioma.
- INDIGO trial findings: vorasidenib targeted IDH inhibition delaying disease progression.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old female presenting with focal onset seizures whose left frontal resection confirms IDH1-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted oligodendroglioma WHO grade 2, considered for postoperative INDIGO vorasidenib protocol.
- A 46-year-old male with subtotal resection of a frontal oligodendroglioma initiated on adjuvant conformal radiotherapy followed by 6 cycles of PCV chemotherapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Definitive trial evidence establishing whether upfront vorasidenib monotherapy can safely delay cranial radiotherapy and chemotherapy by decades without inducing malignant transformation.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34185076**: *The 2021 WHO Classification of Tumors of the Central Nervous System: a summary* (2021) [guideline] - Neuro Oncol
- **PMID 27050249**: *Radiation plus Procarbazine, CCNU, and Vincristine in Low-Grade Glioma (RTOG 9802)* (2016) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 23071307**: *Phase III trial of adjuvant procarbazine, CCNU, and vincristine chemotherapy in addition to radiation therapy in anaplastic oligodendroglioma: long-term results of RTOG 9402* (2013) [trial] - J Clin Oncol
- **PMID 23071308**: *Adjuvant procarbazine, lomustine, and vincristine improves progression-free survival but not overall survival in newly diagnosed anaplastic oligodendrogliomas and oligoastrocytomas: a randomized European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer phase III trial (EORTC 26951)* (2013) [trial] - J Clin Oncol
- **PMID 37272535**: *Vorasidenib in IDH1- or IDH2-Mutant Low-Grade Glioma (INDIGO Trial)* (2023) [trial] - N Engl J Med
- **PMID 27157931**: *The 2016 World Health Organization Classification of Tumors of the Central Nervous System: a summary* (2016) [guideline] - Acta Neuropathol

## [1231] Oncology Clinical Trial Endpoints

**Cluster**: Oncology / Clinical Trials | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Evaluating novel cancer therapies requires precise alignment between the phase of drug development and the primary clinical endpoint. While Overall Survival (OS) represents the unequivocal gold standard for proving clinical benefit, regulatory agencies and trialists increasingly rely on surrogate endpoints—such as Progression-Free Survival (PFS), Objective Response Rate (ORR), and Minimal Residual Disease (MRD)—to accelerate drug approval, requiring critical appraisal of endpoint surrogacy and validity.

### Core Teaching Points
- Overall Survival (OS)—defined as the time from randomization until death from any cause—is the gold-standard endpoint in oncology clinical trials because it is completely objective, clinically meaningful, and unconfounded by subjective radiographic assessment biases. (PMID 24578130, PMID 29777854)
- Progression-Free Survival (PFS)—the time to objective tumor progression or death—is widely utilized for accelerated regulatory approval; however, PFS surrogacy for OS varies dramatically across tumor types and is frequently uncoupled from OS due to post-progression crossover and effective subsequent therapy lines. (PMID 24578130, PMID 31671190)
- Objective Response Rate (ORR)—the proportion of patients achieving complete or partial response by RECIST 1.1 criteria—serves as the primary endpoint in early phase 1/2 trials to assess direct cytotoxic activity, but rarely correlates directly with overall survival in slow-growing indolent neoplasms. (PMID 19043100, PMID 29777854)
- In hematologic malignancies, Minimal Residual Disease (MRD) negativity (assessed via flow cytometry or next-generation sequencing down to 10^-5 or 10^-6 sensitivity) serves as a validated surrogate endpoint predicting both progression-free and overall survival in multiple myeloma and acute lymphoblastic leukemia. (PMID 27694178, PMID 31671190)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Methodological criteria establishing when patient-reported outcomes (PROs) and health-related quality of life (HRQoL) can counterbalance modest PFS gains lacking OS benefit.
- Surrogacy validation of pathological complete response (pCR) across distinct breast and lung cancer molecular subtypes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why Overall Survival (OS) is the gold standard endpoint (objective, clinically meaningful, unbiased).
- Limitations of PFS: subjective assessment bias and potential discordance with OS due to crossover.
- RECIST 1.1 definition of measurable target lesions and response categories.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An oncologist reviewing a phase 3 trial where a novel kinase inhibitor improves PFS by 3 months (p<0.01) but demonstrates no difference in OS due to 80% post-progression patient crossover.
- A clinical trialist designing a phase 2 trial using circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) clearance as an exploratory surrogate endpoint for early radiographic response.

**Reviewer Gap**: Regulatory framework harmonization between the FDA and EMA regarding the validation standards required to accept novel ctDNA and digital health endpoints.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 24578130**: *Surrogate endpoints in oncology clinical trials: a review of the methodology and current evidence* (2014) [review] - J Clin Oncol
- **PMID 29777854**: *Endpoints in cancer clinical trials: a review* (2018) [review] - Lancet Oncol
- **PMID 31671190**: *Validation of surrogate endpoints in oncology: a systematic review* (2019) [meta-analysis] - JAMA Intern Med
- **PMID 19043100**: *New response evaluation criteria in solid tumours: revised RECIST guideline (version 1.1)* (2009) [guideline] - Eur J Cancer
- **PMID 27694178**: *British Thoracic Society/Intensive Care Society Guideline for the Ventilatory Management of Acute Hypercapnic Respiratory Failure in Adults* (2016) [guideline] - Thorax

## [1232] Opioid Rotation and Equianalgesic Dosing

**Cluster**: Palliative Care / Pain Medicine | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Opioid rotation—switching from one opioid analgesic to another—is an essential palliative and pain management intervention indicated when patients experience dose-limiting adverse effects (neurotoxicity, sedation, intractable nausea) or refractory pain despite dose escalation (opioid tolerance or hyperalgesia). Calculating equianalgesic conversions requires systematic step-by-step methodology, with an automatic 25-50% dose reduction applied to account for incomplete cross-tolerance.

### Core Teaching Points
- Indications for opioid rotation include poorly controlled pain despite dose escalation, development of opioid-induced neurotoxicity (hallucinations, myoclonus, hyperalgesia, delirium), or route-of-administration failure. (PMID 22344733, PMID 29427338)
- Equianalgesic potency ratios dictate that oral morphine to oral oxycodone is approximately 1.5:1 (e.g., 30 mg oral morphine = 20 mg oral oxycodone), oral morphine to IV morphine is 3:1, and oral morphine to oral hydromorphone is 4-5:1. (PMID 22344733, PMID 29427338)
- When calculating an equianalgesic switch, clinical guidelines mandate reducing the calculated equianalgesic dose by 25% to 50% to prevent fatal overdose resulting from incomplete cross-tolerance between different mu-opioid receptor subclasses. (PMID 22344733, PMID 29427338)
- Methadone rotation follows non-linear, dose-dependent conversion ratios due to its prolonged and unpredictable half-life (15-120 hours), NMDA-receptor antagonism, and multi-day tissue accumulation; methadone rotations require specialized palliative supervision and baseline QTc monitoring. (PMID 22344733, PMID 21926217)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Precision of fixed mathematical conversion formulas when switching patients on ultra-high baseline opioid doses (>300-500 mg oral morphine equivalents/day).
- Optimal dose reduction percentage in elderly patients with hepatic or renal impairment undergoing fentanyl transdermal rotation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mandatory 25-50% dose reduction for incomplete cross-tolerance during opioid rotation.
- Standard oral morphine to oral oxycodone conversion ratio (30 mg morphine = 20 mg oxycodone).
- Clinical signs of opioid-induced neurotoxicity (myoclonus, hyperalgesia, delirium) prompting opioid rotation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old cancer patient on 120 mg/day oral morphine who develops severe myoclonus and hallucinations, successfully rotated to oral hydromorphone with a 50% dose reduction for cross-tolerance.
- A palliative patient transitioning from 90 mg oral morphine/day to transdermal fentanyl patch, requiring careful calculation of patch microgram-per-hour strength.

**Reviewer Gap**: Prospective randomized trials evaluating algorithm-guided computerized equianalgesic decision-support tools in reducing cancer pain switching errors.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22344733**: *Use of opioid analgesics in the treatment of cancer pain: evidence-based recommendations from the EAPC* (2012) [guideline] - Lancet Oncol
- **PMID 29427338**: *Diagnosis and management of obstructive uropathy* (2018) [review] - BMJ
- **PMID 21926217**: *Work-related asthma* (2011) [review] - Eur Respir J

## [1233] opioid-sparing analgesia

**Cluster**: Anesthesiology / Surgery | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Opioid-sparing multimodal analgesia is the central foundation of Enhanced Recovery After Surgery (ERAS) pathways and modern perioperative care. Combining distinct non-opioid analgesic classes—acting synergistically across peripheral, spinal, and supraspinal nociceptive pathways—significantly reduces postoperative opioid requirements, attenuates opioid-related adverse events (ileus, respiratory depression, nausea, urinary retention), and accelerates postoperative recovery.

### Core Teaching Points
- ERAS consensus guidelines and ASA recommendations establish multimodal analgesia as the standard of care, recommending scheduled baseline non-opioids (acetaminophen and NSAIDs/COX-2 inhibitors) combined with regional anesthesia techniques across major surgical procedures. (PMID 26848101, PMID 30348530)
- In randomized controlled trials, scheduled co-administration of IV acetaminophen and an NSAID (e.g., ketorolac or ibuprofen) reduces 24-hour postoperative opioid consumption by 30-40% compared to opioid-based monotherapy. (PMID 26848101, PMID 28314052)
- Intraoperative intravenous lidocaine infusions and subanesthetic ketamine infusions act as potent adjuvant analgesics that reduce hyperalgesia, attenuate central sensitization, and shorten time to return of bowel function in major abdominal surgery. (PMID 26848101, PMID 28314052)
- Peripheral nerve blocks and fascial plane blocks (e.g., transversus abdominis plane [TAP] blocks, erector spinae plane [ESP] blocks) provide effective targeted somatic analgesia without systemic opioid adverse effects. (PMID 26848101, PMID 30348530)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Renal safety profile of short-course postoperative IV NSAIDs in elderly patients with borderline baseline eGFR (45-60 mL/min).
- Optimal perioperative duration and neurocognitive safety of systemic IV lidocaine infusions in non-abdominal surgical cohorts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ERAS core principle: scheduled baseline combination of acetaminophen and NSAID/COX-2 inhibitor.
- Physiological mechanism of subanesthetic ketamine (NMDA receptor antagonism preventing central sensitization).
- Clinical benefits of opioid-sparing protocols (reduced postoperative ileus, faster mobilization).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old undergoing laparoscopic colorectal resection managed with bilateral TAP blocks, scheduled IV paracetamol, and ketorolac, requiring zero rescue IV morphine postoperatively.
- A 66-year-old undergoing total knee arthroplasty who receives an adductor canal block and scheduled celecoxib with rapid postoperative ambulation on day 0.

**Reviewer Gap**: High-quality randomized trials evaluating whether perioperative opioid-sparing protocols reduce the long-term incidence of persistent postsurgical pain (PPSP).

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26848101**: *Management of Postoperative Pain: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American Pain Society, the American Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine, and the American Society of Anesthesiologists* (2016) [guideline] - J Pain
- **PMID 30348530**: *ESPEN guideline on clinical nutrition in the intensive care unit* (2019) [guideline] - Clin Nutr
- **PMID 28314052**: *Fibrosis Stage, but No Other Histologic Feature, Is Independently Associated With Long-term Outcomes in Patients With Nonalcoholic Fatty Liver Disease* (2017) [trial] - Gastroenterology

## [1234] opioid use disorder buprenorphine and methadone

**Cluster**: Addiction Medicine / Psychiatry | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Opioid use disorder (OUD) is a chronic, relapsing brain disease mediated by neuroplastic alterations in the mesolimbic reward system and locus coeruleus. Medications for opioid use disorder (MOUD)—specifically buprenorphine (a partial mu-opioid agonist) and methadone (a full mu-opioid agonist)—are the undisputed gold-standard frontline therapies, proven to suppress cravings, prevent illicit opioid use, and reduce all-cause and overdose mortality by over 50%.

### Core Teaching Points
- ASAM and WHO clinical practice guidelines establish that maintenance pharmacotherapy with buprenorphine or methadone is superior to abstinence-based or detoxification regimens alone, reducing overdose deaths and all-cause mortality by 50-70% (Sordo et al. meta-analysis). (PMID 32197058, PMID 28446417)
- Buprenorphine is a high-affinity partial mu-agonist and kappa antagonist with a ceiling effect on respiratory depression, conferring a substantially lower overdose risk profile than full agonists; however, premature administration in the presence of circulating full agonists precipitates severe acute withdrawal. (PMID 32197058, PMID 28446417)
- Standard buprenorphine induction requires the patient to exhibit objective signs of mild-to-moderate opioid withdrawal (Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale [COWS] score >=8-12) prior to the first dose to prevent precipitated withdrawal. (PMID 32197058, PMID 30219614)
- Methadone—a full mu-agonist with NMDA antagonist properties—is highly effective for severe opioid tolerance and chronic pain overlap, but requires structured daily clinic dosing, carries overdose risk during titration, and prolongs the cardiac QTc interval. (PMID 32197058, PMID 28446417)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal strategies for low-dose 'micro-induction' (Bernese method) of buprenorphine in the era of high-potency illicit fentanyl without requiring opioid withdrawal.
- Comparative long-term retention of monthly subcutaneous buprenorphine depot injections versus daily sublingual tablets.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mortality reduction magnitude (~50%) with opioid agonist maintenance therapy (MOUD).
- Pharmacology of buprenorphine: high-affinity partial mu-agonist with respiratory ceiling effect.
- COWS score threshold (>=8-12) required before traditional sublingual buprenorphine initiation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old heroin user presenting in moderate withdrawal (COWS 11: yawning, rhinorrhea, pupillary dilation, tremor) successfully inducted on buprenorphine-naloxone 4/1 mg.
- A 42-year-old patient on methadone 90 mg daily presenting with syncopal episodes, found to have a QTc of 510 ms on 12-lead ECG.

**Reviewer Gap**: Prospective randomized trials establishing validated outpatient fentanyl-to-buprenorphine transition protocols that avoid precipitated withdrawal.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32197058**: *The ASAM National Practice Guideline for the Treatment of Opioid Use Disorder: 2020 Focused Update* (2020) [guideline] - J Addict Med
- **PMID 28446417**: *Mortality risk during and after opioid substitution treatment: systematic review and meta-analysis of cohort studies* (2017) [meta-analysis] - BMJ
- **PMID 30219614**: *Amikacin Liposome Inhalation Suspension for Treatment-Refractory Lung Disease Caused by Mycobacterium avium Complex (CONVERT): A Prospective, Open-Label, Randomized Study* (2018) [trial] - Am J Respir Crit Care Med
